Towards a complete classification of non-chiral topological phases in 2D fermion systems
Jing-Ren Zhou, Qing-Rui Wang, Zheng-Cheng Gu

Abstract
In recent years, fermionic topological phases of quantum matter has attracted a lot of attention. In a pioneer work by Gu, Wang and Wen, the concept of equivalence classes of fermionic local unitary(FLU) transformations was proposed to systematically understand non-chiral topological phases in 2D fermion systems and an incomplete classification was obtained. On the other hand, the physical picture of fermion condensation and its corresponding super pivotal categories give rise to a generic mathematical framework to describe fermionic topological phases of quantum matter. In particular, it has been pointed out that in the fermionic string-net models of certain fermionic topological phases, there exists the so-called q-type strings which have no analogues in bosonic theories. In this paper, we generalize the Gu, Wang and Wen construction to include those fermionic topological phases with q-type strings. We argue that all non-chiral fermionic topological phases in 2+1D are characterized by a set of tensors ( N k i ​ j , F k i ​ j , F k ​ l ​ n , χ ​ δ i ​ j ​ m , α ​ β , n i , d i ) (N^{ij}_{k},F^{ij}_{k},F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta},n_{i},d_{i}) , which satisfy a set of nonlinear algebraic equations parameterized by phase factors Ξ k ​ l i ​ j ​ m , α ​ β \Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl} and Ξ k ​ l ​ n , χ ​ δ i ​ j \Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta} . Moreover, consistency conditions among algebraic equations give rise to additional constraints on these phase factors which allow us to construct a topological invariant partition for an arbitrary triangulation of 3D spin manifold. Finally, several examples with q-type strings are discussed, including the Fermionic topological phase from Tambara-Yamagami category for ℤ 2 ​ N \mathbb{Z}_{2N} , which can be regarded as the ℤ 2 ​ N \mathbb{Z}_{2N} parafermion generalization of Ising fermionic topological phase.



I
Introduction

I.1
The goal of this paper

Since the discovery of fractional quantum Hall effect (FQHE)
Tsui et al. 1982
, it has been realized that these peculiar quantum matters can be described by a new type of order—topological order
Wen 1990
. The topological order of FQHE can be characterized by its precise quantization of the Hall conductance, fractionalized charge and fractionalized statistics carried by elementary excitations
Laughlin 1983
. Mathematically, it is well known that topological order in 2D bosonic systems can be systematically described and classified by the advanced mathematical theory –– unitary modular tensor category (UMTC)
Kitaev 2006
. On the other hand, it has also been realized that the patterns of long-range entanglement
Chen et al. 2010
gives rise to an essential physical picture to understand various topological phases. In particular, the equivalence classes of local unitary(LU) transformations
Chen et al. 2010
allows us to construct fixed point wavefunctions to classify all non-chiral topological phases in 2D bosonic systems
Kitaev 2003
;
Levin and Wen 2005
.

Nevertheless, the UMTC framework can not be applied to fermion systems directly. Topological phases in interacting fermion systems are strictly richer
than bosonic systems due to the Fock space structure of fermionic Hilbert space. In addition to the well known FQHE states which are known as chiral topological phases, many new examples of non-chiral topological phases are constructed for 2D fermion systems
Freedman et al. 2004
;
Gu et al. 2014
;
Gu et al. 2015
;
Lan et al. 2016
. Interestingly, it has been shown that a fermionic generalization of Pentagon relation is necessary for understanding topological phases in 2D fermion systems. Very recently, the physical picture of fermion condensation and its corresponding super pivotal categories
Aasen et al. 2019
give rise to a generic mathematical framework to derive the fermionic Pentagon relation
Gu et al. 2015
and understand the underlying physics for almost all non-chiral topological phases in 2D fermion systems. Most surprisingly, it has been pointed out that there are two distinct types of objects in the resulting super fusion categories, and the so-called q-type objects have no analogues in bosonic theories
Aasen et al. 2019
. Nevertheless, it is still unclear how to understand the algebraic relations generated by fermion condensation
Wan and Wang 2017
;
Aasen et al. 2019
;
Lou et al. 2021
from the patterns of long-range entanglement for 2D fermion systems.

In this paper, we aim at generalizing the equivalence classes of fermionic LU(fLU) transformation framework to construct and classify all non-chiral topological phases, including those cases with q-type objects in 2D fermion systems. Then we will try to understand the deep relationship between fermion condensation picture and the equivalence classes of fLU transformations. Below we will briefly review the precise meaning of fermionic topological phases and fLU transformations.

I.2
Gapped quantum liquids

The classification of gapped quantum phases is in general beyond the Landau symmetry breaking paradigm. For bosonic systems, we define that two gapped quantum systems belong to the same equivalence class if they are connected by a sequence of LU transformations without closing the energy gap, and the LU transformations are generated by a finite-time evolution of local Hermitian operators
Hastings and Wen 2005
;
Bravyi et al. 2006
;
Bravyi et al. 2010
;
Chen et al. 2010
:

|\Phi\rangle\sim|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle\text{ iff }|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle=\mathcal{T}e^{i\int d\tau\widetilde{H}(\tau)}|\Phi\rangle,

(1)

where
\mathcal{T}
is the time-ordering operator
and
\widetilde{H}(\tau)=\sum_{i}O_{i}(\tau)
is a summation of local Hermitian operators. Under such a equivalence relation, the trivial phase is connected to the direct-product state, and other nontrivial phases are long-range entangled and called topologically ordered phases.

In discrete spacetime, e.g. on a lattice, the LU transformations can be expressed by a finite depth quantum circuit, generated by piece-wise local unitary operators
U_{pwl}=\prod_{i}U(i)
, where
\{U(i)\}
is a set of unitary operators acting on non-overlapping regions. A quantum circuit with depth
M
is given by:
U^{M}_{circ}=U^{(1)}_{pwl}U^{(2)}_{pwl}...U^{(M)}_{pwl}
. Thus the discrete version of the equivalence relation is written as:

|\Phi\rangle\sim|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle\text{ iff }|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle=U^{M}_{circ}|\Phi\rangle.

(2)

More precisely, in this paper we only consider a subset of gapped quantum phases, namely the gapped quantum liquid (GQL)
Zeng and Wen 2015
which can be defined on arbitrary lattice geometry. In addition, we are also allowed to remove or add degree of freedoms into the systems. Thus, the equivalence classes should be redefined as the generalized local unitary (gLU) transformations satisfying
U_{g}^{\dagger}U_{g}=P
and
U_{g}U_{g}^{\dagger}=P^{\prime}
, where
P
and
P^{\prime}
are two projectors. In particular, the action of
P
does not change the state
|\Phi\rangle
.
In such a way, some of the quantum gapped phases cannot be included, e.g. the fracton topological order
Haah 2011
;
Yoshida 2013
;
Vijay et al. 2015
.

I.3
Fermionic gapped quantum liquids and its classification

In fermionic systems, the underset degrees of freedom are fermions and the total Hilberst space is Fock space instead of a simple tensor product of local Hilbert space. Hence we should redefine the LU transformations as the fermionic LU (fLU) transformations
Gu et al. 2015
:

|\Phi\rangle\sim|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle\text{ iff }|\Phi^{\prime}\rangle=\mathcal{T}e^{i\int dg\widetilde{H}_{f}(g)}|\Phi\rangle,

(3)

which can also be discretized as the fermionic quantum circuits, where the local fermionic Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}_{f}(g)=\sum_{i}\mathcal{O}_{i}(g)
is a summation of pseudo-local bosonic operators
\mathcal{O}_{i}(g)
. Here
\mathcal{O}_{i}(g)
is a product of even number of local fermionic operators (due to the conservation of fermion parity) and any number of local bosonic operators. It is called ”pseudo-local” as it is local for a fermion system in a sense that the fLU transformations acting on different local regions, but are non-local due to the global anti-commutation relation of the fermion creation or annihilation operators.
Similarly, the fLU transformations can also be redefined as the generalized fLU(gfLU) transformations for fermionic GQL(fQGL).
Thus, the fermionic topological orders are classified by the equivalence classes of gfLU transformations
\tilde{U}_{g}
, which are projective unitary operators. Up to some unitary transformations,
\tilde{U}_{g}
is a Hermitian
projection operator:

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{g}

\displaystyle=U_{1}P_{g}U_{2},

\displaystyle P_{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=P_{g},

\displaystyle P_{g}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=P_{g},

\displaystyle U_{1}^{\dagger}U_{1}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle U_{2}^{\dagger}U_{2}

\displaystyle=1.

(4)

We will call such a gfLU transformation a
primitive gfLU transformation. A generic gfLU
transformation is a product of several primitive gfLU
transformations which may contain several hermitian
projectors and unitary transformations, for example,

\tilde{U}_{g}=U_{1}P_{g}U_{2}P^{\prime}_{g}U_{3}
. We note that
\tilde{U}_{g}
contains only even numbers of fermionic operators (i.e.
\tilde{U}_{g}
is a pseudo-local bosonic operator).
We also regard the inverse of
\tilde{U}_{g}
,
\tilde{U}_{g}^{\dagger}
, as a gfLU
transformation. An fLU transformation is viewed as a special
case of gfLU transformations. Clearly
\tilde{U}_{g}^{\dagger}\tilde{U}_{g}=\tilde{P}
and
\tilde{U}_{g}\tilde{U}_{g}^{\dagger}=\tilde{P}^{\prime}
are two Hermitian projectors.

Similar to bosonic systems,
\tilde{U}_{g}
can generate a wavefunction renormalization which allows us to connect the same fGQL state defined on different lattice geometry with different degrees of freedoms. In this paper, by constructing the most generic fixed point wavefucntions from
\tilde{U}_{g}
, we argue that all non-chiral fermionic topological phases in 2D fermion systems are characterized by a set of tensors
(N^{ij}_{k},F^{ij}_{k},F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta},n_{i},d_{i})
, which are data in fermionic string-net models that satisfy a set of nonlinear algebraic equations parameterized by phase factors
\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}
and
\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}
. In particular, in order to to include those fermionic topological phases with q-type objects, the tensor
F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}
must be a gfLU transformation instead of the usual fLU transformation in Gu, Wang and Wen’s construction. In such a way, we reveal the origin of q-type objects and naturally explain why they do not have analogues in bosonic theories from quantum information perspective. Moreover, consistency conditions among algebraic equations give rise to additional constraints on these phase factors which allow us to construct a topological invariant partition for an arbitrary triangulation of 3D spin manifold.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows: In section
II
, we construct the most general fixed-point wavefunction for non-chiral fermionic topological orders in 2D. Then we derive the conditions for all wavefunction renormalization moves with the inclusion of q-type strings, i.e., the conditions on
F
-move,
O
-move,
Y
-move,
H
-move, and dual
F
/
H
-move. Thus we obtain a set of most general algebraic equations in section
II.11
.
In section
III
, we explicit construct the topological invariant partition function for an arbitrary triangulation of 3D spin manifold. We find that the relations among phase factors for constructing the partition function can be obtained from the fermionic Pentagon equation and four projective unitary conditions for
F
-move. These relations match with the results from fermion condensation theory
Aasen et al. 2019
, as illustrated in section
B.5
. In section
IV
, several examples with q-type strings are studied, including the fermionic topological phase from Tambara-Yamagami category for
\mathbb{Z}_{2N}
, which can be regarded as the
\mathbb{Z}_{2N}
parafermion generalization of Ising fermionic topological phase. Finally, we summarized this work in section
V
.

In Appendix
A
, we review some basic concepts in super pivotal category introduced in Ref.
Aasen et al. 2019
. Appendix
B
introduces the explict steps to do fermion condensation. We apply the fermion condensation scheme to derive several equivalence relations on fixed-point states with q-type strings, and derive all fermionic
F
-symbols for the four examples from their corresponding bosonic theory. In Appendix
C
, we define a special sequence of moves, whose equivalence relation gives the phase factor
\Delta^{mji,\alpha\delta}_{nl}
, which is involved in the relations among phase factors needed for constructing the partition function. Appendix
D
is a proof that all possible 2-3 moves induced by time-ordering are consistent with the four projective unitary conditions as well as the relations among the corresponding phase factors.

II
Wavefunction renormalization for generic non-chiral topological phases in 2D fermion systems

II.1
Fixed-point wavefunctions on a graph

Since the wave-function renormalization may change the
lattice structure, we will consider quantum state defined on a
generic trivalent graph G with a branching structure such that each vertex has two incoming or one incoming edges.
Similar to the construction of string-net model for bosonic systems, we assume each edge has
N+1
states, labeled by
i=0,...,N
. Each
vertex also has physical states. The string fusion rules and the local fermion parity are both encoded in the vertex states
\alpha=1,...,N^{ij}_{k}
or
\beta=1,...,N_{ij}^{k}
, where
N^{ij}_{k}(N_{ij}^{k})
is the number of fusion states with two incoming (outgoing) strings
i,j
and one outgoing(incoming) string
k
, graphically represented as
\begin{matrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ingoing}\end{matrix}
or
\begin{matrix}\includegraphics[scale]{outgoing}\end{matrix}
. Generally, we have

N^{ij}_{k}=B^{ij}_{k}+F^{ij}_{k},

(5)

where
B^{ij}_{k}
is the number of bosonic fusion states, and
F^{ij}_{k}
is the number of fermionic fusion states (a local fermion excitation is involved), represented as a solid dot
\begin{matrix}\includegraphics[scale]{fusionf}\end{matrix}
. We introduce a number
s(\alpha)
to indicate the vertex states is bosonic or fermionic:
s(\alpha)=0
if the state
\alpha
is bosonic and
s(\alpha)=1
if it is fermionic.

In this paper, we will assume that

\displaystyle N^{ij}_{k}=N_{ij}^{k},\ \ \ \ B^{ij}_{k}=B_{ij}^{k},\ \ \ \ F^{ij}_{k}=F_{ij}^{k},

(6)

as required by unitarity. Our fixed-point state is
a superposition of those basis states

\displaystyle|\psi_{\text{fix}}\rangle=\sum_{\text{all conf.}}\psi_{\text{fix}}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{strnet}}}\right)\left|\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{strnet}}}\right\rangle.

(7)

In the bosonic string-net models, there is a very strong assumption
that the above graphic states on two graphs are the same if
the two graphs have the same topology. However, since different
vertices and edges are distinct and a generic graph
state does not have such a topological invariance. Similar as the construction in Ref.
Gu et al. 2015
, here we will consider
vertex-labeled graphs (v-graphs) where each vertex is
assigned an index
\underline{\alpha}
. Two v-graphs are said to be
topologically the same if one graph can be continuously
deformed into the other in such a way that vertex labelings
of the two graphs matches.

II.2
The structure of fixed-point wavefunctions

Firstly, we need to divide the state on each edge into m-type and q-type strings. When all strings
i,j,k
are m-type, generally
B^{ij}_{k}
is not equal to
F^{ij}_{k}
, however, when there is a q-type string involved in the fusion (at least two strings in
i,j,k
are q-type), we must have

B^{ij}_{k}=F^{ij}_{k}
(The physical reason of such an assumption will be explained below). Thus we can introduce the function
B(\alpha)=1,...,B^{ij}_{k}
to extract the bosonic fusion state of
\alpha
:

B(\alpha)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha\text{, \ \ if }s(\alpha)=0\\
\alpha-B^{ij}_{k}\text{, \ \ if }s(\alpha)=1\end{array}\right.

(8)

We note that
B(\alpha)
is only defined when q-type strings are involved in
i,j,k
. Here we introduce the notation
\cdot f
to denote the changing of fermion parity without changing the corresponding bosonic state, i.e.,
B(\alpha\cdot f)=B(\alpha)
.

Now let us consider the fixed-point wavefunctions on a patch
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
, where the boundary string states
i,j,k,l
are fixed, while yellow shaded ellipse means that the inner fusion states
\alpha,\beta
and the inner string state
m
may vary. (More precisely,
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
should be regarded as function
\phi_{ijkl,\Gamma}(\alpha,\beta,m)
where the indices
on the other part of the graph are summarized by
\Gamma
.)
All such fixed-point wavefunctions(as functions of
\alpha,\beta,m
) form a linear space called the support space
V^{ijk}_{l}
, whose dimension is called the support dimension
D^{ijk}_{l}
.

For the fixed-point wavefunctions
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
, the number of inner states
\{\alpha,\beta,m\}
is
N^{ijk}_{l}=\sum_{m}N^{ij}_{m}N^{mk}_{l}
. Specially, if the inner string
m
is a q-type string, the support space
V^{ijk}_{l}
should mod out the following equivalence relations generated by string
m
, in fermion parity-even and odd sectors respectively:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g1}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4n}\end{pmatrix},

(9)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g3n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g2n}\end{pmatrix},

(10)

which can be altogether denoted as:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix},

(11)

where
f
denotes a transparent (local) fermion excitation, and
\times f
means changing the fermion parity on a fusion state via attaching a transparent fermion, which does not have to preserve the original bosonic state in general, i.e.
B(\alpha\times f)
generally may not be the same as
B(\alpha)
. However, we require
(\alpha\times f)\times f=\alpha
as attaching a pair of transparent fermions should not affect the fusion state on each vertex. Here the equivalence relation
\sim
is up to a phase. Physically, the first relation means that a pair of transparent fermions can be created or annihilated on q-type strings (that’s why we must require
B^{ij}_{k}=F^{ij}_{k}
once q-type string is involved in the fusion), and the second relation means that a local fermion excitation can slide along q-type strings freely.
Thus, we can assign a number
n_{i}
to each string, with
n_{i}=1
for a m-type string and
n_{i}=2
for a q-type string.
Mathematically,the number
n_{i}
can be regarded as the dimension of the endomorphism algebra of string
i
, as explained in Appendix
A
. As a result, the support dimension
D^{ijk}_{l}
is generally equal to or less than the number of inner states
N^{ijk}_{l}
, and

D^{ijk}_{l}=\underset{m}{\sum}\frac{N^{ij}_{m}N^{mk}_{l}}{n_{m}}.

(12)

Similarly, the support space of the fixed-point wavefunctions on
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2gAA}\end{pmatrix}
should also mod out the following equivalence relation if
n
is a q-type string:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4n}\end{pmatrix},

(13)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g3n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g2n}\end{pmatrix},

(14)

which can be in together denoted as:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4fff}\end{pmatrix},

(15)

where similarly the changing of fermion parity
\times f
here does not have to preserve the original bosonic fusion state. The support dimension here is

D^{ijk}_{l}=\underset{n}{\sum}\frac{N^{in}_{l}N^{jk}_{n}}{n_{n}}.

(16)

II.3
F
-move

The first type of wavefunction renormalization is the
F
-move, which is a gfLU transformation between the two fixed-point wavefunctions
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
and
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2gAA}\end{pmatrix}
(We assume that the two fixed point wavefucntions are the same for the other parts of the graph).

Apparently, the support dimensions on both sides are equal:

\underset{m}{\sum}\frac{N^{ij}_{m}N^{mk}_{l}}{n_{m}}=\underset{n}{\sum}\frac{N^{in}_{l}N^{jk}_{n}}{n_{n}}.

(17)

Since the fermion parity-odd sector and the parity-even sector are independent, this relation can be further split as

\underset{m}{\sum}\frac{B^{ij}_{m}B^{mk}_{l}+F^{ij}_{m}F^{mk}_{l}}{n_{m}}=\underset{n}{\sum}\frac{B^{in}_{l}B^{jk}_{n}+F^{in}_{l}F^{jk}_{n}}{n_{n}},

(18)

\underset{m}{\sum}\frac{B^{ij}_{m}F^{mk}_{l}+F^{ij}_{m}B^{mk}_{l}}{n_{m}}=\underset{n}{\sum}\frac{B^{in}_{l}F^{jk}_{n}+F^{in}_{l}B^{jk}_{n}}{n_{n}}.

(19)

In fact, for fermionic systems, the total Hilbert space is a Fock space, and we need to specify the ordering of the fermionic states in the fixed point wavefunctions. For example,
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
actually means
\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta},...}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
where the fermionic state on vertex
\underline{\beta}
is always created before the fermionic state on vertex
\underline{\alpha}
. An elegant way to count the ordering of fermionic states is to introduce
the Majorana numbers
\theta_{\underline{\alpha}},\theta_{\underline{\beta}},...
, where
\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta},...
denote the vertices carrying the fusion state
\alpha,\beta,...
. The Majorana numbers satisfy:

\displaystyle\theta_{\underline{\alpha}}^{2}=1,\ \ \ \ \theta_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta_{\underline{\beta}}=-\theta_{\underline{\beta}}\theta_{\underline{\alpha}}\text{ for any }\alpha\neq\beta,

\displaystyle\theta_{\underline{\alpha}}^{\dagger}=\theta_{\underline{\alpha}},\ \ \ \ \ (\theta_{\underline{\alpha}}...\theta_{\underline{\beta}})^{\dagger}=\theta_{\underline{\beta}}...\theta_{\underline{\alpha}}.

(20)

Thus, we can define the ordering independent fixed-point wavefunctions
\Psi_{\text{fix}}
by attaching Majorana numbers on
\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta},...}
.

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta},...}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}

(21)

Similarly, for the patch
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2gAA}\end{pmatrix}
, we can also define:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}=\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\chi}\underline{\delta},...}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}.

(22)

where in
\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\chi}\underline{\delta},...}
the fermionic state on vertex
\underline{\delta}
is always created before the fermionic state on vertex
\underline{\chi}
.

On the other hand, since the fermion ordering in
\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta},...}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
and
\psi_{\text{fix}}^{\underline{\chi}\underline{\delta},...}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2gAA}\end{pmatrix}
will be naturally induced by the branching structure of the graph, below we will also omit the subscript
{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta},...}
and
{\underline{\chi}\underline{\delta},...}
throughout the whole paper without confusion. However, one should always keep in mind that
\Psi_{\text{fix}}
is the Majorana number valued ordering independent wavefunction while
\psi_{\text{fix}}
is the ordering dependent fixed-point wavefunctions.

Similar to the Gu, Wang and Wen construciton, we can introduce a Majorana number valued
\mathcal{F}
-move without specifying the ordering of fermions on vertices of both patches:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix},

(23)

where

\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta},

(24)

which follows our Majorana number convention in Eq.(
21
) and Eq.(
22
). The
\mathcal{F}
-move is non-zero only when all the fusion states are non-vanishing and the fermion parity is conserved:
s(\alpha)+s(\beta)+s(\chi)+s(\delta)=0\text{ mod }2
. Or in other words,

\displaystyle F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}=0\text{ when }N^{ij}_{m}<1\text{ or }N^{mk}_{l}<1\text{ or }N^{jk}_{n}<1

\displaystyle\text{ or }N^{in}_{l}<1,\text{or }s(\alpha)+s(\beta)+s(\chi)+s(\delta)=1\text{ mod }2.

(25)

Here the complex number valued
F
-symbol is defined according to the special fermion ordering scheme discussed above. The unique advantage of the Majorana number valued
\mathcal{F}
-move is that the anti-commuting nature of fermion creating/annhilation operators is naturally encoded in such a gFLU and we do not need to worry about the fermion ordering problem when considering a sequence of gFLU transformations.

However, very different from the Gu, Wang and Wen construciton where
\mathcal{F}
-move is assumed to be unitary, here if
m
is q-type, we can only fix the target space up to a certain superposition of two equivalent states in the 1-dimensional projective space:
\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}
and
\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix}
. Therefore, depending on whether
m
is an m-type or q-type string, the
\mathcal{F}
-move can be unitary or projective unitary. In particular, when
m
is q-type, the projective unitary condition should map to both of the two equivalent states, and we require:

\underset{n\chi\delta}{\sum}F^{ijm^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}_{kln,\chi\delta}(F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }m\text{ is m-type
}\\
\frac{1}{n_{m}}(\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}+\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{(\alpha\times f)\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{(\beta\times f)\beta^{\prime}})\text{, \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right.

(26)

We note that the Majorana numbers cancel out due to relation in Eq.(
20
), and we can write down the projective unitary condition for the complex valued
F
-moves without Majorana numbers. Here
\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}
is a phase factor satisfying
(\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl})^{*}=\Xi^{ijm,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{kl}
. It depends on strings
i,j,k,l,m
and fusion states
\alpha,\beta
. We should note that
B(\alpha\times f)
and
B(\beta\times f)
do not have to be the same as
B(\alpha)
and
B(\beta)
in general. But the explicit corresponding bosonic fusion state of
\alpha\times f
can be determined by
\alpha
and the three strings
i,j,k
attached to it. Similarly,
B(\beta\times f)
can be determined by
\beta
and strings
m,k,l
. If
m
is q-type,
this projective unitary condition can be viewed as the following projective map:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{(\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl})^{*}}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix},

(27)

\displaystyle\frac{\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix},

(28)

If we view both
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1gAA}\end{pmatrix}
and
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2gAA}\end{pmatrix}
as column basis vectors in each support space,
the above expression can also be rewritten in matrix form:

P=F^{ijk}_{l}(F^{ijk}_{l})^{\dagger},

(29)

where
P
is a projective matrix with the following form:

P=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{(\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl})^{*}}{2}\\
\frac{\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right),

(30)

Apparently, it satisfies
P^{2}=P
.

From Eq.(
27
) and Eq.(
28
), we see that the phase factor
\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}
is actually the phase difference between the two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix}=\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kl}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type.}

(31)

According to the
F
-move (with the aforementioned fermionic state ordering convention), we have:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g4fff}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}F^{ijm,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{kln,\chi\delta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}

(32)

and

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}.

(33)

Comparing each term with fixed
n,\chi,\delta
, we immediately obtain a relation between the
F
-moves on two equivalent states:

\displaystyle F^{ijm,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{kln,\chi\delta}=\Xi^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln}F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}\text{, \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type.}

(34)

If we reverse the initial space and target space,
we can obtain the inverse fermionic
\mathcal{F}
-move:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{m\alpha\beta}(\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F1g}\end{pmatrix}.

(35)

If
n
is q-type, we can only fix the target space up to certain superposition of the two equivalent states in Eq.(
13
) or Eq.(
14
), and there will be another independent projective unitary condition for
F
-move (Similarly the Majorana numbers cancel out so that we can write down the relation for
F
-move):

\underset{m\alpha\beta}{\sum}(F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}})^{*}F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{\delta\delta^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }n\text{ is m-type
}\\
\frac{1}{n_{n}}(\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{\delta\delta^{\prime}}+\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{(\chi\times f)\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{(\delta\times f)\delta^{\prime}})\text{, \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right.

(36)

where
\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}
is another phase factor satisfying
(\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}=\Xi^{ij}_{kln,(\chi\times f)(\delta\times f)}
. It depends on strings
i,j,k,l,n
and fusion states
\chi,\delta
.
B(\chi\times f)
and
B(\delta\times f)
do not have to be the same as
B(\chi)
and
B(\delta)
respectively in general. If
n
is q-type, this projective unitary condition can be viewed as the following projective map:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{(\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4fff}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix},

(37)

\displaystyle\frac{\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g1}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4fff}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4fff}\end{pmatrix},

(38)

In terms of matrix form, we have:

P^{\prime}=(F^{ijk}_{l})^{\dagger}F^{ijk}_{l},

(39)

where the projective matrix takes the form:

P^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{(\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}}{2}\\
\frac{\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}}{2}&\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right),

(40)

which also satisfies
(P^{\prime})^{2}=P^{\prime}
.

The phase factor
\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}
is actually the phase difference between these two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g4fff}\end{pmatrix}=\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{F2g}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type.}

(41)

from which we can obtain another relation between the
F
-moves on two equivalent states:

\displaystyle(F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,(\chi\times f)(\delta\times f)})^{*}=\Xi^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}(F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}\text{, \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type}.

(42)

II.4
Fermionic pentagon equation

Similar to the Gu, Wang and Wen construction, if we apply the gFLU transformations on a bigger patch of the graph, certain consistent condition is required.
The so-called fermionic pentagon equation is essentially a consistency relation on two paths connecting two fixed point wavefunctions:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent1g}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{t\eta\psi}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{knt,\eta\psi}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent4g}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{t\eta\psi;s\kappa\gamma}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{knt,\eta\psi}\mathcal{F}^{itn,\psi\chi}_{lps,\kappa\gamma}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent5g}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{t\eta\kappa;\psi;s\kappa\gamma;q\delta\phi}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{knt,\eta\psi}\mathcal{F}^{itn,\psi\chi}_{lps,\kappa\gamma}\mathcal{F}^{jkt,\eta\kappa}_{lsq,\delta\phi}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent3g}\end{pmatrix},

(43)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent1g}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{q\delta\epsilon}\mathcal{F}^{mkn,\beta\chi}_{lpq,\delta\epsilon}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent2g}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{q\delta\epsilon;s\phi\gamma}\mathcal{F}^{mkn,\beta\chi}_{lpq,\delta\epsilon}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\epsilon}_{qps,\phi\gamma}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{pent3g}\end{pmatrix},

(44)

which leads to:

\underset{\epsilon}{\sum}\mathcal{F}^{mkn,\beta\chi}_{lpq,\delta\epsilon}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\epsilon}_{qps,\phi\gamma}\simeq\underset{t\eta\psi\kappa}{\sum}\mathcal{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{knt,\eta\psi}\mathcal{F}^{itn,\psi\chi}_{lps,\kappa\gamma}\mathcal{F}^{jkt,\eta\kappa}_{lsq,\delta\phi}.

(45)

By eliminating the Majorana numbers and canceling out the constant phase factors via a proper phase shift of the
F
-symbol, we can use a constant phase factor to change
\simeq
into
=
:

\underset{\epsilon}{\sum}F^{mkn,\beta\chi}_{lpq,\delta\epsilon}F^{ijm,\alpha\epsilon}_{qps,\phi\gamma}=(-1)^{s(\alpha)s(\delta)}\underset{t\eta\psi\kappa}{\sum}F^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{knt,\eta\psi}F^{itn,\psi\chi}_{lps,\kappa\gamma}F^{jkt,\eta\kappa}_{lsq,\delta\phi}.

(46)

II.5
O
-move

The second type of wavefunction renormalization is the
O
-move,
graphically expressed as:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ioip}\end{pmatrix}\simeq O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}.

(47)

We only permit parity-even
O
-move, i.e.,

O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}=0\text{ when }N^{ij}_{k}<1\text{ or }s(\alpha)+s(\beta)=1\text{ mod }2.

(48)

The support space of
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip}\end{pmatrix}
should also mod out the following equivalence relation when
k
is q-type:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O1}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O4}\end{pmatrix}.

(49)

We still use the convention to assign Majorana numbers from top to bottom and define the fermin ordering independent wavefunction as:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O1}\end{pmatrix}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O1}\end{pmatrix}.

(50)

and rewrite the ordering independent
\mathcal{O}
-move as:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ioip}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{O}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}.

(51)

where the fermionic
\mathcal{O}
-move is defined as:

\mathcal{O}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k},

(52)

However, the
O
-move itself is not a gFLU transformation in general, as in the patch
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip}\end{pmatrix}
, fermion parity-odd states actually exist when
k
is q-type, but we only permit parity-even
O
-move. Therefore, we should define a three-vertices
\widetilde{O}
-move as a gFLU transformation, which includes the following six different cases:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip31}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{1}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline31}\end{pmatrix},

(53)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip32}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{2}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline32}\end{pmatrix},

(54)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip33}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline33}\end{pmatrix},

(55)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip34}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{4}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline34}\end{pmatrix},

(56)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip35}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{5}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline35}\end{pmatrix},

(57)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iOip36}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{O_{6}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline36}\end{pmatrix},

(58)

where in all cases the state
\lambda
is related to
\gamma
by:

\lambda=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\gamma\text{, \ \ if }s(\alpha)+s(\beta)=0\\
\gamma\times f\text{, \ \ if }s(\alpha)+s(\beta)=1\text{ and }k\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right..

(59)

Different from the original
O
-move, when
k
is q-type, our newly defined
\widetilde{O}
-move permits parity-odd sector, i.e.
s(\alpha)+s(\beta)=1
. In this case, the additional fermion is moved to the third vertex, as a fermion can slide on a q-type string. This is the reason why we have
\lambda=\gamma\times f
when
s(\alpha)+s(\beta)=1\text{ and }k\text{ is q-type}
.

We define the fermionic three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-move as

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\gamma)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\lambda)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}\text{, \ \ for }a=1,2,3,

(60)

and

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}_{a}}^{ij,\gamma\alpha\beta}_{kpq,\lambda}=\theta^{s(\gamma)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\lambda)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\gamma\alpha\beta}_{kpq,\lambda}\text{, \ \ for }a=4,5,6.

(61)

Since the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
-move is a gFLU transformation, after cancelling out the Majorana numbers, it must satisfy:

\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}(\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda})^{*}=1\text{, \ \ for }a=1,2,3,4,5,6.

(62)

From Eq.(
62
), depending on whether
k
is m-type or q-type, we have the following conditions for
O
-move:

(1) When
k
is m-type,

\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}(O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}=1,

(63)

This is because
\widetilde{O}
is identical to
O
-move when
k
is m-type.

(2) When
k
is q-type, we divide Eq.(
62
) in parity-even and odd sectors:

\displaystyle\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}(\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda})^{*}\delta_{s(\alpha)s(\beta)}

\displaystyle+\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}(\widetilde{O_{a}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda})^{*}\delta_{s(\alpha)(s(\beta)+1)}=1.

(64)

For parity-even sector
s(\alpha)=s(\beta)
, the three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-move just equals to the corresponding
O
-move. While for parity-odd sector
s(\alpha)=s(\beta)+1
, the three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-move differs from the corresponding
O
-move by sliding a fermion or creating two fermions on a q-type string, which can at most cause a phase difference(see more detailed computations for all
\widetilde{O}
-move below). Thus we can replace the three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-move by the original
O
-move, where in the parity-odd sector the general phase factor difference cancels out, we finally get:

2\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}(O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}=1.

(65)

Combining Eq.(
63
) and Eq.(
65
), the original
O
-move satisfies:

n_{k}\underset{ij\alpha\beta}{\sum}O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}(O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}=1,

(66)

We stress that since the parity-odd states actually exist when
k
is q-type, there is also a equivalence relation:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O3}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O2}\end{pmatrix},

(67)

Physically, as a fermion can slide freely on a q-type string, we can move the fermion outside the patch and still apply the parity-even
O
-move. Such a scheme may only cause a phase factor difference, which is exactly achieved by our newly defined three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-move.

We define
\Lambda^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}
as the phase difference of the two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{O4fff}\end{pmatrix}=\Lambda^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ioip}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type,}

(68)

from which we have a relation between the
O
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle O^{ij,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{k}=\Lambda^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}\text{, \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type},

(69)

where the phase factor
\Lambda^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}
generally depends on strings
i,j,k
and fusion states
\alpha,\beta
. The phase factor has the property
(\Lambda^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}=\Lambda^{ij,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{k}
.

II.6
Y
-move

The third type of wavefunction renormalization is the
Y
-move, which is a completeness condition relating
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y}\end{pmatrix}
to
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ijline}\end{pmatrix}
. Similarly when
k
is q-type, the support space of
\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y}\end{pmatrix}
should mod out the following equivalence relations:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y1}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y4}\end{pmatrix}.

(70)

As the
Y
-move exists as a completeness condition, we can always assume that in the above two equivalent states, the changing of fermion parity never changes the bosonic fusion states. In this paper, we denote a changing of fermion parity that
may
change the bosonic state as
\times f
, and denote a changing of fermion parity that never changes the bosonic state as
\cdot f
(The definition of
\cdot f
is enclosed in the definition of
\times f
).

When
k
is m-type, the completeness condition is graphically expressed as:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\alpha\beta}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ijline}\end{pmatrix}.

(71)

Specially, when
k
is q-type, it is written as:

\displaystyle\sum_{kB(\alpha)B(\beta)}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}(\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{YB}\end{pmatrix}+(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{YfffB}\end{pmatrix})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \simeq\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{ijline}\end{pmatrix},

(72)

where the weight coefficient
Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}
should count for the pair of two equivalent states, and the summation is over all bosonic states of
\alpha
and
\beta
.
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}
is defined as the phase difference of the two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Yfff}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{Y}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type,}

(73)

from which we have a relation between the
Y
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)(\beta\cdot f)}=(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta})^{*}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}\text{, \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type},

(74)

where
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}
is also a phase factor satisfying
(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta})^{*}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)(\beta\cdot f)}
.

However, since we have Eq.(
74
),
Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k})^{*}
can be rewritten as
Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)(\beta\cdot f)}
, and Eq.(
72
) can be still written in the form of Eq.(
71
). But we should keep in mind that when
k
is q-type, the two equivalents states are always paired in counting weights in the completeness condition.

Similar to
\mathcal{O}
-move, we can also define
the fermionic
\mathcal{Y}
-move as:

\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}=\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}.

(75)

II.7
A gauge freedom and a relation between
O
-move and
Y
-move

There is a gauge freedom in the bosonic states in the support space
V^{ij}_{k}
, i.e. we can do the following transformation on the fixed-point wavefunctions:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{gauge1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{B(\beta)}u^{ij,B(\alpha)}_{k,B(\beta)}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{gauge2}\end{pmatrix},

(76)

where
u^{ij}_{k}
is a unitary matrix.

Therefore, since we only permit parity-even
O
-move, i.e. the fermion parity on the two vertices are always the same, we can make a gauge choice on the
O
-move such that the basis choices in the fusion space
V^{ij}_{k}
and the splitting space
V^{k}_{ij}
are always the same, i.e.

O^{ij,\alpha\beta}_{k}=O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\delta_{\alpha\beta}

(77)

Under such a gauge, Eq.(
69
) is written as:

\displaystyle O^{ij,(\alpha\times f)}_{k}=\Lambda^{ij,\alpha}_{k}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\text{, \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type.}

(78)

Similarly, we can make the same gauge choice on
Y
-move:

Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha\beta}=Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\beta},

(79)

and Eq.(
74
) can also be simplified as:

\displaystyle Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}=(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha})^{*}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\text{, \ \ if }k\text{ is q-type.}

(80)

There is a relation between some ordering-independent
\mathcal{O}
-moves and a
\mathcal{Y}
-move. We discuss in two cases. Depending on
k
is m-type or q-type, we have:

(1) If
k
is m-type,

\displaystyle\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{k^{\prime}\beta}\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k^{\prime},\beta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY2}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY3}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\beta}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\beta}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix},

(81)

we have:

1=Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k},

(82)

where we can choose the constant phase of
Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}
such that
\simeq
is replaced by
=
.

(2) If
k
is q-type,

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{k^{\prime}\beta}\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k^{\prime},\beta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY2}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY3}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{Y}^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY4}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\beta}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\beta}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY6}\end{pmatrix}+\theta^{s(\alpha\cdot f)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha\cdot f)}_{\underline{\beta}}Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha\times f)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\alpha\times f)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha(\alpha\cdot f)(\alpha\cdot f)}_{kij,\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY6}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}+\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha(\alpha\cdot f)(\alpha\cdot f)}_{kij,\alpha}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\gamma}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq 2\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\lambda}}Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{kline}\end{pmatrix},

(83)

where
\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha(\alpha\cdot f)(\alpha\cdot f)}_{kij,\alpha}
is related to
O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}
by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha(\alpha\cdot f)(\alpha\cdot f)}_{kij,\alpha}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY6}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY4}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY3}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OY6}\end{pmatrix}.

(84)

We note that when we derive the relation on equivalent states, we can consider the fixed-point wavefunctions without Majorana numbers
\psi_{\text{fix}}
. But when we derive relations among different renormalization moves, we should always consider fermionic fixed-point wavefuntions
\Psi_{\text{fix}}
attached with Majorana numbers.

From Eq.(
80
) and Eq.(
84
), we have
Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}=Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha(\alpha\cdot f)(\alpha\cdot f)}_{kij,\alpha}
. Therefore we obtain

1=2Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}.

(85)

where we also choose the convention to eliminate the phase difference on both side. Replacing the
Y
-move and
O
-move by the equivalence relations in Eq.(
78
) and Eq.(
80
), we get:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij}_{k,\alpha}Y^{ij}_{k,(\alpha\cdot f)}=\frac{1}{2(\Lambda^{ij,\alpha}_{k})^{*}O^{ij,(\alpha\times f)}_{k}},

(86)

where we note that generally
\alpha\cdot f
is different from
\alpha\times f
, as generally the bosonic states can be changed in the equivalence relations of
O
-move.

Combining the two cases that
k
is m-type or q-type, we obtain:

Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}=\frac{1}{n_{k}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}}.

(87)

In addition, from derivation in Eq.(
84
), we see that
\widetilde{O_{1}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}
and
\widetilde{O_{6}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}
are related:

\widetilde{O_{3}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}=\widetilde{O_{6}}^{ij,\beta\alpha\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda},

(88)

II.8
Dual
F
-move and a relation between
O
-move and
F
-move

We can also define a fermionic dual
\mathcal{F}
-move as the following local projective unitary transformation:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{m\alpha\beta}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF2}\end{pmatrix},

(89)

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}=\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\widetilde{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}.

(90)

When
n
is q-type, we define
\Xi^{{}^{\prime}ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}
as the phase difference of these two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF1ff}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\Xi}^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF1}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type,}

(91)

from which we have another relation between the dual
F
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,(\chi\times f)(\delta\times f)}=\widetilde{\Xi}^{ij}_{kln,\chi\delta}\widetilde{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta}\text{, \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type}.

(92)

When
m
is q-type, we define
\widetilde{\Xi}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln}
as the phase difference of these two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF2ff}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\Xi}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dualF2}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type,}

(93)

from which we have a relation between the dual
F
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{F}^{ijm,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{kl,\chi\delta})^{*}=\widetilde{\Xi}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln}(\widetilde{F}^{ijm,\alpha\beta}_{kln,\chi\delta})^{*}\text{, \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type}.

(94)

There is a relation between
O
-move,
F
-move and dual
F
-move. On one hand, depending on string
p
is m-type or q-type, we have:

(1) If
p
is m-type,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{p^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF2}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF6}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}O^{km,\chi}_{p}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{km,\chi}_{p}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix},

(95)

where in the second line the Majorana numbers
\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}=(-1)^{s(\alpha)+s(\chi)}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}
.

(2) If
p
is q-type,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{p^{\prime}\chi^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{pp^{\prime}}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF2}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF6}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF7}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}O^{km,\chi}_{p}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF4}\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi\times f)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\alpha\times f)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\widetilde{O_{1}}^{km,\chi(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}_{pji,\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF4}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}O^{km,\chi}_{p}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\theta^{s(\alpha\times f)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi\times f)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\chi\times f)}_{\underline{\chi^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\alpha\times f)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}\widetilde{O_{1}}^{km,\chi(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}_{pji,\alpha}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha^{\prime}}}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq 2\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{km,\chi}_{p}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix},

(96)

where
\widetilde{O_{1}}^{km,\chi(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}_{pji,\alpha}
is related to
O^{km,\chi}_{p}
by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{O_{1}}^{km,\chi(\chi\times f)(\alpha\times f)}_{pji,\alpha}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF4}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF7}\end{pmatrix}=\Xi^{jk}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF6}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\Xi^{jk}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{km,\chi}_{p}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF4}\end{pmatrix}.

(97)

Combining with the case that
p
is m-type, the general result is given by:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq n_{p}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{km,\chi}_{p}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix},

(98)

On the other hand, depending on string
l
is m-type or q-type, we have:

(1) If
l
is m-type,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{l^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF3}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF9}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}.

(99)

(2) If
l
is q-type,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{l^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF3}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF9}\end{pmatrix}+\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl,(\mu\times f)(\tau\times f)}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF10}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF5}\end{pmatrix}+\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{jkl,(\mu\times f)(\tau\times f)}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\tau\times f)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu\times f)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\widetilde{O_{5}}^{jk,(\tau\times f)(\mu\times f)\mu}_{lmi,\tau}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF5}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu\times f)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau\times f)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,(\mu\times f)(\tau\times f)}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\theta^{s(\tau\times f)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu\times f)}_{\underline{\mu^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\widetilde{O_{5}}^{jk,(\tau\times f)(\mu\times f)\mu}_{lmi,\tau}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq 2\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix},

(100)

where
\widetilde{O_{5}}^{jk,(\tau\times f)(\mu\times f)\mu}_{lmi,\tau}
is related to
O^{jk,\mu}_{l}
by:

\displaystyle\widetilde{O_{5}}^{jk,(\tau\times f)(\mu\times f)\mu}_{lmi,\tau}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF5}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF10}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\Xi}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF9}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\widetilde{\Xi}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF5}\end{pmatrix}.

(101)

Combining with the case that
p
is m-type, the general result is given by:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{OF1}\end{pmatrix}\simeq n_{l}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\mu)}_{\underline{\mu}}\theta^{s(\tau)}_{\underline{\tau}}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}O^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{iline}\end{pmatrix}.

(102)

Therefore, from Eq.(
98
) and Eq.(
102
), we have the relation:

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}=\frac{n_{p}}{n_{l}}F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}O^{km,\chi}_{p}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}(O^{lm,\tau}_{i})^{-1}(O^{jk,\mu}_{l})^{-1},

(103)

from which we obtain two relations on phase factors, if we change the fermion parity on
\mu,\tau
through Eq.(
34
), Eq.(
78
) and Eq.(
94
), and on
\chi,\alpha
through Eq.(
42
), Eq.(
78
) and Eq.(
92
) respectively:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi}=\Lambda^{lm,\tau}_{i}\Lambda^{jk,\mu}_{l}(\Xi^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi})^{*},

(104)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}^{jk}_{mip,\chi\alpha}=\Lambda^{km,\chi}_{p}\Lambda^{jp,\alpha}_{i}(\Xi^{jk}_{mip,\chi\alpha})^{*}.

(105)

We require the dual
F
-move
\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}
also to be projective unitary:

\underset{p\chi\alpha}{\sum}(\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha})^{*}\widetilde{F}^{jkl^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}_{mip,\chi\alpha}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }l\text{ is m-type
}\\
\frac{1}{n_{l}}(\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}+(\widetilde{\Xi}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi})^{*}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{(\mu\times f)\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{(\tau\times f)\tau^{\prime}})\text{, \ \ if }l\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right.

(106)

Replacing Eq.(
103
) into Eq.(
106
), and by Eq.(
104
),

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \underset{p\chi\alpha}{\sum}(\frac{n_{p}}{n_{l}})^{2}(F^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha})^{*}F^{jkl^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}_{mip,\chi\alpha}\frac{\left|O^{km,\chi}_{p}O^{jp,\alpha}_{i}\right|^{2}}{(O^{lm,\tau}_{i})^{*}O^{l^{\prime}m,\tau^{\prime}}_{i}(O^{jk,\mu}_{l^{\prime}})^{*}O^{jk,\mu^{\prime}}_{l}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }l\text{ is m-type
}\\
\frac{1}{n_{l}}(\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{\tau\tau^{\prime}}+(\Lambda^{lm,\tau}_{i})^{*}(\Lambda^{jk,\mu}_{l})^{*}\Xi^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mi}\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{(\mu\times f)\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{(\tau\times f)\tau^{\prime}})\text{, \ \ if }l\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right.

We see that in the above equation, when
l
is q-type, we have

\frac{1}{(O^{lm,\tau}_{i})^{*}O^{l^{\prime}m,\tau^{\prime}}_{i}(O^{jk,\mu}_{l^{\prime}})^{*}O^{jk,\mu^{\prime}}_{l}}=(\Lambda^{lm,\tau}_{i})^{*}(\Lambda^{jk,\mu}_{l})^{*}\frac{1}{\left|O^{lm,\tau}_{i}O^{jk,\mu}_{l}\right|^{2}}\text{, \ \ when }\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{(\mu\times f)\mu^{\prime}}\delta_{(\tau\times f)\tau^{\prime}}=1.

(109)

Then Eq.(
II.8
) can be satisfied by the following ansatz for
O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}
:

O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}=\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\sqrt{\frac{d_{i}d_{j}}{n_{i}n_{j}n_{k}D^{2}d_{k}}}\delta^{ij}_{k},

(110)

where
D^{2}=\underset{i}{\sum}\frac{d^{2}_{i}}{n_{i}}
, and
\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k}
is a general phase factor. And Eq.(
II.8
) reduces to the projective unitary condition for
F
-move in Eq.(
26
).

By Eq.(
87
), the
Y
-move has the expression:

Y^{ij}_{k,\alpha}=(\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k})^{*}\sqrt{\frac{n_{i}n_{j}D^{2}d_{k}}{n_{k}d_{i}d_{j}}}\delta^{ij}_{k}.

(111)

Since we have Eq.(
78
) and Eq.(
80
), the phase
\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k}
must satisfy:

\Phi^{ij,(\alpha\times f)}_{k}=\Lambda^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k},

(112)

and

\Phi^{ij,(\alpha\cdot f)}_{k}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha}_{k}\Phi^{ij,\alpha}_{k},

(113)

where we note that the
\alpha\times f
in Eq.(
112
) is determined by the corresponding states in equivalent
O
-moves. Specially, when the fermion parity change in equivalent
O
-moves also does not change the bosonic state, Eq.(
112
) and Eq.(
113
) reduce to the same equation. In this special case, we have
\Lambda^{ij,\alpha}_{k}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{ij,\alpha}_{k}
.

After taking the gauge on
O
-move in Eq.(
77
), Eq.(
66
) becomes:

n_{k}\underset{ij\alpha}{\sum}O^{ij,\alpha}_{k}(O^{ij,\alpha}_{k})^{*}=1.

(114)

Combining with Eq.(
110
), we find the quantum dimensions satisfy:

\underset{ij}{\sum}\frac{N^{ij}_{k}d_{i}d_{j}}{n_{i}n_{j}}=d_{k}D^{2}.

(115)

From derivations in Eq.(
97
), and Eq.(
101
), we have more relations between different three-vertex
\widetilde{O}
-moves:

\widetilde{O_{1}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}=\widetilde{O_{4}}^{ij,\beta\alpha\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda},

(116)

\widetilde{O_{2}}^{ij,\alpha\beta\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}=\widetilde{O_{5}}^{ij,\beta\alpha\gamma}_{kpq,\lambda}.

(117)

The other projective unitary condition for dual
F
-move is:

\underset{l\mu\tau}{\sum}\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip,\chi\alpha}(\widetilde{F}^{jkl,\mu\tau}_{mip^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}})^{*}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\delta_{pp^{\prime}}\delta_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }p\text{ is m-type
}\\
\frac{1}{n_{p}}(\delta_{pp^{\prime}}\delta_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}+(\widetilde{\Xi}^{jk}_{mip,\chi\alpha})^{*}\delta_{pp^{\prime}}\delta_{(\chi\times f)\chi^{\prime}}\delta_{(\alpha\times f)\alpha^{\prime}}\text{, \ \ if }p\text{ is q-type}\end{array}\right.

(118)

Replacing Eq.(
103
) into Eq.(
118
), and by Eq.(
105
), we can similarly reduce it to the projective unitary condition in Eq.(
36
) with the ansatz in Eq.(
110
).

II.9
H
-move and an additional constraint between dual
H
-move and
F
-move

We define the fermionic
\mathcal{H}
-move as the following local projective unitary transformation:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H2}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}\mathcal{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H1}\end{pmatrix},

(119)

where

\mathcal{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}=\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}H^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}.

(120)

Similarly, the fermionic dual
H
-move is defined as

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H3}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H1}\end{pmatrix},

(121)

where

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}=\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\widetilde{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}.

(122)

Again, when
m
is a q-type string, there exist the following equivalence relations:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh1n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh4n}\end{pmatrix},

(123)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh2n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh3n}\end{pmatrix}.

(124)

When
n
is a q-type string, there exist the following equivalence relations:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy1n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy4n}\end{pmatrix},

(125)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy2n}\end{pmatrix}\sim\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy3n}\end{pmatrix}.

(126)

For convenience, below we will show how to derive the dual
H
-move from the combination of
F
,
Y
and
O
-moves first, and the projective unitarity condition of
H
-move will impose additional conditions on
F
-symbol.
When
m
is q-type, we define
\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jl}
as the phase difference of these two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh4fff}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jl}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dh1}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type,}

(127)

where in general the bosonic states
B(\alpha\times f)
and
B(\beta\times f)
may not be the same as
B(\alpha)
and
B(\beta)
respectively. Thereby we have a relation between the dual
H
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{kim,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{jln,\chi\delta}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jl}\widetilde{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta}\text{, \ \ if }m\text{ is q-type},

(128)

where
\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jl}
satisfies
(\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jl})^{*}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{kim,(\alpha\times f)(\beta\times f)}_{jl}
.

When
n
is q-type, we define
\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,\chi\delta}
as the phase difference between these two equivalent states:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy4fff}\end{pmatrix}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,\chi\delta}\psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{dy1}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type,}

(129)

from which we have another relation between the dual
H
-moves of two equivalent states:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,(\chi\times f)(\delta\times f)})^{*}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,\chi\delta}(\widetilde{H}^{kim,\alpha\beta}_{jln,\chi\delta})^{*}\text{, \ \ if }n\text{ is q-type},

(130)

where
\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,\chi\delta}
satisfies
(\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,\chi\delta})^{*}=\widetilde{\zeta}^{ki}_{jln,(\chi\times f)(\delta\times f)}
.

There is a relation between the dual
H
-move and
F
-move. Depending on string
j
is m-type or q-type, we have

(1) If
j
is m-type,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H3}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{n\delta}\mathcal{Y}^{kl}_{n,\delta}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H4}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\delta j^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}\chi}\mathcal{Y}^{kl}_{n,\delta}\mathcal{F}^{imk,\alpha\delta}_{lnj^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\chi}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H5}\end{pmatrix}\simeq\sum_{n\delta\chi}\mathcal{Y}^{kl}_{n,\delta}\mathcal{F}^{imk,\alpha\delta}_{lnj,\beta\chi}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H8}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta^{\prime}}}Y^{kl}_{n,\delta}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta^{\prime}}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta^{\prime}}}F^{imk,\alpha\delta}_{lnj,\beta\chi}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta^{\prime}}}O^{ml,\beta}_{j}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\simeq\sum_{n\chi\delta}\theta^{s(\beta)}_{\underline{\beta}}\theta^{s(\alpha)}_{\underline{\alpha}}\theta^{s(\delta)}_{\underline{\delta}}\theta^{s(\chi)}_{\underline{\chi}}Y^{kl}_{n,\delta}F^{imk,\alpha\delta}_{lnj,\beta\chi}O^{ml,\beta}_{j}\Psi_{\text{fix}}\begin{pmatrix}\includegraphics[scale]{H1}\end{pmatrix},

(131)

(2) If
j
is q-type,
